(43)

The fundamental theorem for semi-injective PEPS
Molnar et al. 2018
states that the action of an on-site symmetry
g\in G
on the physical legs in the region
\mathcal{R}
transforms into the action on the virtual legs in the boundary
\partial\mathcal{R}
as

\displaystyle U_{g}(\mathcal{R})A(\mathcal{R})=A(\mathcal{R})V_{g}(\partial\mathcal{R})

(44)

where
V_{g}(\partial\mathcal{R})
is an MPO acting on the virtual legs in
\partial\mathcal{R}
, as visualized in Fig.
7
(a)
12
12

12

More precisely, in
Molnar et al. 2018
the
V_{g}(\partial\mathcal{R})
was defined on the Hilbert space of the minimal rank decomposition of four-qubit state
\psi
in semi-injective PEPS. If the projection from the PEPS bond Hilbert space to this Hilbert space is
P
, one can always find a pseudoinverse
P^{+}
(not unique) to bring
V_{g}(\partial\mathcal{R})
into the PEPS bond Hilbert space.
.
For a horizontal strip
\mathcal{R}
,
V_{g}(\partial\mathcal{R})=V_{g}(\partial\mathcal{R}_{\textrm{top}})\cdot V^{-1}_{g}(\partial\mathcal{R}_{\textrm{bot}})
consists of two parts acting on top and bottom boundaries. Note that the operations on the top and bottom boundaries cancel each other such that the symmetry action on a larger strip
\mathcal{R}
still results in the virtual action on its two boundaries.
One can obtain a similar behavior for a vertical strip.
We would use
V_{g}
to denote a single local tensor for an MPO
V_{g}(\partial\mathcal{R})
.

Figure 7:

(a) In a semi-injective PEPS, the action of an on-site symmetry
U_{g}
on a strip
R
transforms into the action of an MPO on the boundaries
V_{g}
(bottom) and
V_{g}^{-1}
(top).
(b) For a given 2d local tensor
\mathbb{A}
, one contract two physical legs (red lines). The transfer matrix
\mathbb{T}
on an infinite cylinder can be obtained by contracting it along the circumference. This transfer matrix captures correlation along the infinite direction. (c) Given two MPO symmetries
V_{g_{1}}
and
V_{g_{2}}
, one can reduce them into
V_{g_{1},g_{2}}
by applying reduction tensors
P_{g_{1},g_{2}}
and
P_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{+}
from its left and right. (d) Two different ways to merge three symmetry MPOs from one end give rise to a relative phase factor
\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
.

With these definitions in hand, we can extend the concept from pure states to two-dimensional tensor-network density operators (TNDO) as follows:

Definition VIII.2
.

(Strong semi-injective TNDO) Consider a TNDO
\rho[\mathbb{A}]
with local tensor
\mathbb{A}
with two physical and four virtual indices. It is strong semi-injective if

1.

The doubled state
|\rho[\mathbb{A}]\rangle\!\rangle
for the TNDO is a semi-injective PEPS, where
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}[\mathbb{\psi},\mathbb{O}]
.

2.

The transfer matrix
\mathbb{T}
of the TNDO on the infinite cylinder is defined by contracting local tensor
\sum_{i}\mathbb{A}^{ii}
(two superscripts are physical indices for ket and bra) as in Fig.
7
(b).
\mathbb{T}
has a unique largest eigenvalue at any circumference length
L
, and the corresponding eigenvector is given as an injective MPS with a finite bond dimension.

These two conditions are the direct generalizations of the conditions for MPDO.
The first condition suggests the density matrix represents a short-range entangled state within a doubled Hilbert space.
The second condition ensures that the correlations are short-range within the physical Hilbert space. If the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbb{T}
is not unique, there exists either a local or loop operator with a long-range correlation along the cylinder’s axis, which deviates from the intended short-range correlated criteria.

A natural consequence of this definition is that the non-degenerate onsite quantum channel preserves strong semi-injectivity.
This is because a non-degenerate onsite channel acts on physical Hilbert space at each site as an injective map, thus its action can be absorbed into an invertible map
\mathbb{O}
. Moreover, its action cannot change the transfer matrix
\mathbb{T}
.

Equipped with Eq. (
44
), one can study how MPO symmetries are fused or decomposed. As MPO symmetries
\{V_{g}|g\in G\}
should form a representation of
G
, given two MPO symmetries
V_{g_{2}}
and
V_{g_{1}}
, there exists a three-leg
reduction
tensor
P_{g_{1},g_{2}}
(as a surjective map from two virtual bonds to one virtual bond) and its pseudo-inverse
P^{+}_{g_{1},g_{2}}
such that it merges
V_{g_{1}}
and
V_{g_{2}}
into
V_{g_{2}g_{1}}
.
Taking further steps, one can imagine the composition of three MPO symmetries. At each end, there are two different ways to compose
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
as illustrated in Fig.
7
(c), and this leads to the phase factor
\omega
(c.f. Eq. (
10
)):

\displaystyle P_{g_{1},g_{2}g_{3}}{}^{g_{1}}\!P_{g_{2},g_{3}}

\displaystyle=\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})P_{g_{1}g_{2},g_{3}}P_{g_{1},g_{2}},

(45)

where superscript on the left of
P_{g_{2},g_{3}}
corresponds to a complex conjugation if
g_{1}
is anti-unitary
Else and Nayak 2014
;
Chen et al. 2011c
.
Note that the merging operation for
P^{+}
on the left of the local MPO tensor produces the relative phase factor
\omega^{-1}
, which cancels the phase factor from merging
P
.
The 3-cocycle
\omega
is defined up to a gauge transformation as one can always redefine
P_{g,h}\mapsto\varphi(g,h)P_{g,h}
and
P^{+}_{g,h}\mapsto\varphi^{*}(g,h)P^{+}_{g,h}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\mapsto\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\frac{\varphi(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})\varphi(g_{1},g_{2})}{\varphi(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3}){}^{g_{1}}\!\varphi(g_{2},g_{3})}.

(46)

Furthermore, by considering two different reduction procedures for four MPO symmetries, we can arrive at the following consistency equation [c.f. Eq. (
11
)]:

\displaystyle\frac{\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\omega(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3},g_{4}){}^{g_{1}}\!\omega(g_{2},g_{3},g_{4})}{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3},g_{4})\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}g_{4})}=1.

(47)

As this is exactly the 3-cocycle condition,
\omega
is given as a particular equivalence class of the third cohomology group
\mathcal{H}^{3}(G,\mathrm{U}(1))

Chen et al. 2011c
.

Note that local tensors for vertical and horizontal MPO symmetries can form a projective representation:
V_{g}V_{h}=\lambda(g,h)V_{gh}
. This projective representation can be different for vertical and horizontal MPOs, each of which corresponds to a weak SPT protected by the translation
T_{x}
and
T_{y}
, respectively. However, for an onsite symmetry group with a finite order,
\lambda^{n}=1
for some
n
. Accordingly, if we regroup
n
sites along
x
or
y
direction,
\lambda\rightarrow\lambda^{n}=1
and this weak SPT structure can be trivialized. On the other hand, the equivalence class of
\omega
is stable under regrouping multiple sites into a single site, thus
[\omega]
provides a scale-invariant labeling of a semi-injective PEPS
Molnar et al. 2018
.

VIII.2
Classification

Next, we classify the SPT phases of strong MPO-injective TNDO protected by a direct product of the strong symmetry
G
and the weak symmetry
K
.
Again, we formulate the density operator as the doubled state and investigate the SPT phases of the doubled state subject to the physical constraints of the density matrix similar to Sec.
V
.
We focus on the
strong
SPTs as in Ref.
Cheng et al. 2016
characterized by the third group cohomology of internal symmetries.
The
weak
SPT states in two dimension
Cheng et al. 2016
, which are a stack of 1d SPT states protected by the internal and translational symmetries, are constrained by the constraints discussed in Sec.
V
.

The doubled state
|\rho\rangle\!\rangle
of the strong injective TNDO exhibits a symmetry
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{G}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
, which consists of the physical symmetry
\mathbb{G}_{p}=G_{l}\times G_{r}\times K
and the hermiticity symmetry
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
, same as its 1d counterpart.
In two dimensions, the possible SPT phases are classified by the third cohomology group
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{G},\mathrm{U}_{T}(1))
.

To begin, the anti-unitary
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
cannot protect a nontrivial 2d SPT phase by itself
Chen et al. 2013
.
Moreover, as shown in Appendix
D.2
, one can pick a gauge of the reduction operator such that the 3-cocycle involving Hermiticity symmetry is trivial, i.e.
\omega(\mathbb{H},\cdot,\cdot)=\omega(\cdot,\mathbb{H},\cdot)=\omega(\cdot,\cdot,\mathbb{H})=1
.
This indicates the absence of mixed anomaly between
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{p}
.

The SPT phases are therefore only protected by the physical symmetry and are classified by the third cohomology group
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{G}_{p},\mathrm{U}(1))
.
According to the Künneth decomposition,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{G}_{p},\mathrm{U}(1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{3}(G_{l},\mathrm{U}(1))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{3}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{3}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))

\displaystyle\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{2}(K,\mathrm{U}(1)))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{r},\mathcal{H}^{2}(K,\mathrm{U}(1)))

\displaystyle\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(K,\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{l},\mathrm{U}(1)))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(K,\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1)))

\displaystyle\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1)))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{r},\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{l},\mathrm{U}(1)))

\displaystyle\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(K,\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1)))).

(48)

As shown in Appendix
C
, each component in the decomposition has a faithful representation given by the slant product.
In what follows, we discuss the constraints on the possible phases due to the Hermiticity, semi-positivity, and strong injectivity of the density operator.

VIII.2.1
Absence of SPT phase protected by weak symmetry

\mathbb{T}

(b):

The strong injectivity of the TNDO requires that the eigenvector associated with the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbb{T}
is in the form of injective MPS

\displaystyle|\phi[C])=\hbox to143.27pt{\vbox to22.34pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.72638pt\lower-7.61319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

(50)

The global symmetry transformation
U_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}
with
k\in K
on the 2d state does not change the transfer matrix
\mathbb{T}
; the transformations on the ket and the bra Hilbert space cancel after tracing over physical legs.
We thus have

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}=V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}^{-1}\mathbb{T}V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}.

(51)

This indicates that
|\phi[C])
is symmetric under the projective MPO symmetry, i.e.
V_{{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}}
acting on
|\phi[C])
produces a global phase,
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}|\phi[C])=e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}}|\phi[C])
.
Hence, according to Ref.
Molnar et al. 2018
;
Chen et al. 2011c
, there exists a reduction
Q_{{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}}
such that

\displaystyle\hbox to74.99pt{\vbox to52.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.95276pt\lower-9.0359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.93124pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(52)

The above property indicates that
|\phi[C])
is symmetric under the weak symmetry transformation.
Thus,
|\phi[C])
is non-anomalous under the weak symmetry, suggesting that the weak symmetry cannot protect nontrivial SPT phases by itself.
Specifically, consider local tensors
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}},V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}},V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
associated with the MPO for weak symmetry.
The reduction among the three MPOs and the eigenstate
|\phi[C])
satisfies a pentagon equation
Chen et al. 2011c
,

Q_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
Q_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}}
Q_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}}
Q_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
P_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}}
Q_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}}
P_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}}
P_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
Q_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
P_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
Q_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
Q_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}}
P_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
P_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
Q_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
C
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
C
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
C
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
C
\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})
\omega(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})
\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})
\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2})
\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}}
V_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3}}
C
\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})
\omega(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})
\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})
\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2})
\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})

For simplicity, we only draw the reduction on the right of the local tensors.
The relation indicates

\displaystyle\omega(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})=\frac{\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})}{\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})\varphi(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2})}.

(53)

Thus, the 3-cocycle
\omega(\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{1},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{2},\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}_{3})
is coboundary, i.e. no SPT phases protected by the weak symmetry.

VIII.2.2
Constraints from Hermiticity

The Hermiticity of the density matrix constrains the possible SPT phases protected by
\mathbb{G}_{p}
by requiring the 3-cocycle to satisfy

\displaystyle\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2},\mathsf{g}_{3})=\omega^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{3}),

(54)

where
\overline{\mathsf{g}}:=\mathbb{H}\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H}
. This can be shown using the 3-cocycle condition in Eq. (
47
).

This relation puts constraints on the slant product

\displaystyle[\omega(\mathsf{g}^{l}_{1},\mathsf{g}^{l}_{2},\mathsf{g}^{l}_{3})]

\displaystyle=-[\omega(\mathsf{g}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{g}^{r}_{2},\mathsf{g}^{r}_{3})],

(55)

\displaystyle[i_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1}^{l},\mathsf{g}_{2}^{l})]

\displaystyle=-[i_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1}^{r},\mathsf{g}_{2}^{r})]

(56)

\displaystyle[i_{\mathsf{k}_{1}^{\mathrm{w}}}i_{\mathsf{k}_{2}^{\mathrm{w}}}\omega(\mathsf{g}^{l})]

\displaystyle=-[i_{\mathsf{k}_{1}^{\mathrm{w}}}i_{\mathsf{k}_{2}^{\mathrm{w}}}\omega(\mathsf{g}^{r})].

(57)

VIII.2.3
Constraints from semi-positivity

Here, we show the 2d SPT phases involving the mixed anomaly between the left and the right symmetry cannot exist due to the semi-positivity of the density matrix.
In the Künneth decomposition, these phases correspond to
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1)))
,
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{r},\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{l},\mathrm{U}(1)))
, and
\mathcal{H}^{1}(K,\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1))))
.

To begin, the SPT phases in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1)))
has a faithful representation in terms of the slant product (as shown in Appendix
C
)

\displaystyle i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2}):=\frac{\omega(\mathsf{g}^{l},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2})\omega(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2},\mathsf{g}^{l})}{\omega(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{g}^{l},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2})}.

(58)

The slant product is a 2-cocycle, and its cohomology class
[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1))
.
At the same time, it is a one-dimensional representation of
G^{l}
, i.e.
i_{\mathsf{g}_{1}^{l}}\omega\;i_{\mathsf{g}_{2}^{l}}\omega=i_{\mathsf{g}_{1}^{l}\mathsf{g}_{2}^{l}}\omega
.
Hence,
[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1)))
.

The SPT state characterized by a non-trivial slant product
i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega
exhibits a membrane order parameter

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega],\mathsf{g}^{l}}(\mathcal{R}):=U_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}(\mathcal{R})\otimes W_{[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]}(\partial\mathcal{R}),

(59)

where
U_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}(\mathcal{R})
is the partial symmetry transformation on region
\mathcal{R}
, and
W_{[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]}(\partial\mathcal{R})
is an entangler of 1d SPT in class
[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]
on its boundary.
The membrane operator acquires a perimeter-law decaying expectation value in the doubled state with an increasing size of region
\mathcal{R}
.

To show this, we consider a partial symmetry transformation
U_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}(\mathcal{R})
in the region
\mathcal{R}
(upper half-plane), which fractionalizes into an MPO
V_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}(\partial\mathcal{R})
on the virtual bond.
As we will see, the MPO
V_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}(\partial\mathcal{R})
acts like a one-dimensional SPT entangler.
Consider the symmetry transformations
U_{\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2}}
followed by
U_{\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1}}
.
As
V_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}
is already inserted along
\partial\mathcal{R}
, these symmetry transformations translate into a sequence of MPO symmetries
V_{\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1}}V_{\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2}}V_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}V_{\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2}}^{-1}V_{\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1}}^{-1}
, which can be reduced in two different ways as the following:

\displaystyle\hbox to68.22pt{\vbox to144.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.94366pt\lower-72.20053pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
;
;
;
;
;

\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;\Lambda(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2},\mathsf{g}^{l}),

(60)

where

\displaystyle\Lambda(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2},\mathsf{g}^{l}):=\frac{\omega(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2}\mathsf{g}^{l},(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2})^{-1})\omega(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2},\mathsf{g}^{l})}{\omega(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1}\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2}\mathsf{g}^{l},(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1})^{-1},(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2})^{-1})}.

(61)

One can show that the phase difference with and without the MPO
V_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}
inserted is given by the slant product (up to a coboundary term), i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2},\mathsf{g}^{l})}{\Lambda(\mathsf{h}^{r}_{1},\mathsf{h}^{r}_{2},\mathsf{e})}=i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega(\mathsf{h}_{1}^{r},\mathsf{h}_{2}^{r}).

(62)

Hence, the MPO
V_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}
transforms as a 1d SPT state
labeled by
[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]
.

For the doubled state of TNDO represented by a semi-injective PEPS, one can always pull the MPO
V_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}
to the Hilbert space of nearby physical qubits
13
13

13

This is because the MPO
V_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}
acts on the Hilbert space of the minimal rank decomposition of four-qubit state
\psi
in semi-injective PEPS. The operator in this space is mapped injectively to the physical Hilbert space
Molnar et al. 2018
. Under the MPO-injectivity condition, the injective portion of
V_{g}
can be still pulled onto the physical space
Şahinoğlu et al. 2021
;
Williamson et al. 2016
.
, i.e. there always exists a 1d MPO
\tilde{W}^{-1}
such that

(63)

Hence, the symmetry transformation
U_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}
decorated with
\tilde{W}
acquires a unit expectation value,
\langle\!\langle\tilde{W}(\partial\mathcal{R})U_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}(\mathcal{R})\rangle\!\rangle\,{=}\,1
.
The MPO
\tilde{W}
is invertible as
V_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}
is invertible; its inverse creates an SPT in class
-[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]
. Furthermore, as
\mathsf{g}^{n}\,{=}\,1
for some finite
n
,
\tilde{W}^{n}\,{=}\,1
on the physical wavefunction, and the eigenvalues of
\tilde{W}
are of unit magnitude.

To further proceed, we want to find an MPO that satisfies the three conditions: (1) It is an SPT entangler purely supported on the right Hilbert space that creates the 1d SPT state labeled as
[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]
, (2) its MPO bond dimension is finite, and (3) its overlap per site with
\tilde{W}
is finite, i.e., the overlap with
\tilde{W}(\mathcal{C})
decays as a perimeter law. The general recipe to construct such an MPO is the following. First, we find a unitary operator
U
acting on the Hilbert space of a left physical site
\mathcal{H}^{l}_{i}
such that
W\,{:=}\,\tr_{\mathcal{H}^{l}_{i}}(\tilde{W}U^{\dagger})\neq 0
as illustrated in Fig.
8
(a). Then, the MPO
U\otimes W
in Fig.
8
(b) must have the same MPO bond dimension as
\tilde{W}
and creates the same SPT state as the symmetry action by
G_{r}
on this MPO translates into the same gauge transformation as
\tilde{W}
. Furthermore, one can always find
U
where
W\neq 0
, ensuring the overlap between
U\otimes W
and
\tilde{W}
is finite per site.
We thus show the existence of a membrane order parameter

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega],\mathsf{g}^{l}}(\mathcal{R})=[U(\partial\mathcal{R})\cdot U_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}(\mathcal{R})]\otimes W_{[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]}(\partial\mathcal{R}),

(64)

which decays with a perimeter law.

Figure 8:

(a) Membrane order parameter with a perimeter law decay on shaded (blue) region implies that the correlation between two loops
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is constant with the separation
y_{2}-y_{1}
. (b) The
\mathsf{g}^{l}\in G_{l}
symmetry flux insertion through the cylinder
F_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}
corresponds to the introduction of a
\mathsf{g}^{l}
-defect along the red line, which has a nontrivial commutation relation with
W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C})
.

To further proceed, consider a cylindrical geometry with circumference size
L
. The existence of a membrane order parameter on the shaded region in Fig.
8
(c) decaying with perimeter-law in the doubled state indicates the long-range correlation following the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, which is similar to the derivation of Eq. (
V.2.3
):

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{1})\;W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{2})\rangle\!\rangle\xrightarrow{|y_{2}-y_{1}|\to\infty}\text{const.}

(65)

where
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is the loop around the cylinder at the location
y_{i}
.
With this, there are two possible scenarios for the behavior of
W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C})
:

(i)
Non-vanishing connected correlation: if the expectation value
\langle\!\langle W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{y})\rangle\!\rangle
does not match its correlation in the infinite separation limit, there is a long-ranged connected correlation of the string operator
W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{y})
, which contradicts the uniqueness of the largest eigenvalue of the transfer matrix as in Def.
VIII.2
.

(ii)
Vanishing connected correlation: in this case, the expectation value
\langle\!\langle W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{y})\rangle\!\rangle
matches the correlation in the infinite separation limit. Accordingly, there is a perimeter-law decaying loop operator
W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C})
that has a mixed anomaly with
G_{r}
and
G_{l}
. This is because
W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C})
creates 1d SPT states labeled by
[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]
on the left Hilbert space and
-[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]
on the right Hilbert space, and such entangler with finite MPO bond dimension should break the symmetry
G_{r}
and
G_{l}
locally
14
14

14

For example, a finite-depth circuit creating 1d
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
cluster SPT state can be constructed as the product of controlled-Z gates, which respects the symmetry on the periodic boundary condition as a whole, but breaks the symmetry on the open boundary condition at each end.
.

As a crucial step, we introduce the flux insertion operator
F_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}
along the cylinder, which gives rise to a
\mathsf{g}^{l}
(or
\mathsf{g}^{r}
) symmetry defect along the vertical line on the cylinder surface
Barkeshli et al. 2019
, see Fig.
8
(b). Such a line of
\mathsf{g}^{l}
-defect translates into the gauge transformation associated with
\mathsf{g}^{l}
on the virtual legs crossed by the line. Consider a sequence of flux insertion operators
\{F_{\mathsf{g}_{i}}\}
such that
\prod_{i}\mathsf{g}_{i}=1
but its projective representation in
[i_{\mathsf{g}^{l}}\omega]
generates
\prod_{i}V_{\mathsf{g}_{i}}=e^{i\eta}\neq 1
. As the doubled state
|\rho\rangle\!\rangle
is symmetric under
G_{l}
, if the doubled state is the unique symmetric ground state of a gapped parent Hamiltonian, the sequence of symmetry flux insertions that trivializes should map the state into itself, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}|\rho\rangle\!\rangle=e^{i\phi}|\rho\rangle\!\rangle
where
\mathcal{F}=\prod_{i}F_{\mathsf{g}^{l}_{i}}
.
\mathcal{F}
further satisfies the following relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\cdot[W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C})]=e^{i\eta}[W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C})]\cdot\mathcal{F}

(66)

since the line defect that crosses with
W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C})
at a single point must give rise to a phase factor associated with the projective representation.
However, plugging in
\mathcal{F}|\rho\rangle\!\rangle=e^{i\phi}|\rho\rangle\!\rangle
to Eq. (
66
), the constant expectation value of
\langle\!\langle W\otimes W^{*}(\mathcal{C})\rangle\!\rangle
implies that
e^{i\eta}=1
, a contradiction to the presence of the anomaly.
We thus argue the absence of SPT phases in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1)))
, and similarly for the phases in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{r},\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{l},\mathrm{U}(1)))
.

Next, we consider a non-trivial SPT phase in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(K,\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{r},\mathrm{U}(1))))
.
Such an SPT phase is characterized by a decorated membrane operator in the doubled state

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{[i_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}\omega],\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}:=U_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathcal{R})\otimes W_{[i_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}\omega]}(\partial\mathcal{R}),

(67)

Here, the weak symmetry transformation
U_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}
acts from both left and right on the density matrix and preserves the positivity of the density matrix, so is
W_{[i_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}\omega]}
.
However, when acting on a symmetric product state,
W_{[i_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}\omega]}
creates an SPT state with mixed anomaly between the left and the right symmetry.
As shown in Sec.
V.2.3
, the resulting SPT state violates the positivity of the density matrix, indicating that
W_{[i_{\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{w}}}\omega]}
cannot be a positive map.
Thus, there cannot be such SPT phases.

VIII.2.4
Summary

To summarize, the Hermiticity and semi-positivity of the density matrix constrain the possible SPT phases in the doubled state of semi-injective TNDO, The resulting classification is given by
\mathcal{H}^{3}(G_{l},\mathrm{U}(1))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{2}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{1}(K,\mathrm{U}(1)))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{l},\mathcal{H}^{2}(K,\mathrm{U}(1)))
, which agrees with that obtained for average SPT in Ref.
Ma and Wang 2023
.

To close, we comment on the constraints of possible doubled-state 2d SPT phases protected by strong 1-form and 0-form symmetry.
In this case, it remains unclear regarding the proper tensor network density operator that represents such states.
However, one can still constrain the mixed anomaly between the left and the right symmetry based on the semi-positivity of the density matrix.
Since the 1-form symmetry cannot protect SPT phases alone
Zhu et al. 2019
, the only possible mixed anomaly involving 1-form symmetry is between the left (right) 0-form symmetry and the right (left) 1-form symmetry.
Phases with such anomaly would feature the string order given by partial 1-form symmetry decorated by 0-form charge, which according to the inequality in Eq. (
V.2.3
), would imply long-range charge correlation, contradicting the assumption of short-range correlation.

We leave the generalization of our results to a broader class of 2d mixed states as well as the rigorous study of possible SPT mixed states protected by higher-form symmetries to future study.

IX
Discussion

In this work, we introduce a broad class of one-dimensional mixed states described by strong injective MPDO, generalizing the concept of injective MPS.
We define and classify the SPT phases in the strong injective MPDO
\rho
protected by both strong and weak symmetry according to the cohomology class of projective representation in the corresponding doubled state.
In the strongly symmetric MPDO that admits a local purification, we show that the SPT phases have a compatible definition based on the equivalence class of mixed states under non-degenerate local channels.
Finally, we extend our framework to study SPT phases in two-dimensional mixed states, where we identify a strong semi-injective TNDO as a suitable generalization, and classify the possible SPT phases.

Notably, our definition of the mixed-state SPT phases is different from the definition based on the “two-way” connection
Coser and Pérez-García 2019
;
Ma and Wang 2023
;
Sang et al. 2023
: two mixed states
\rho_{1,2}
are in the same SPT phase if there exist symmetric local quantum channels
\mathcal{N}_{1,2}
such that
\rho_{1}=\mathcal{N}_{2}[\rho_{2}]
and
\rho_{2}=\mathcal{N}_{1}[\rho_{1}]
.
Instead, we define the equivalence classes of symmetric mixed states based on a simpler “one-way” connection by a
non-degenerate
symmetric channel.
The non-degenerate property of the local channel guarantees that the output state
\mathcal{N}[\rho]
retains the signatures of the anomaly in the input state
\rho
, distinguishing it from SPT states in other phases.
We justify this simplified equivalence relation by proving that for the locally purifiable strong injective MPDOs, the equivalence class under the “one-way” connection reproduces the cohomology classification.
Whether the equivalence class under the two-way connection produces a definition of SPT phases compatible with the definitions in this work is left for future research.

The mixed-state SPT phases in this work are preserved under non-degenerate local quantum channels and characterized by observables in a single-copy density matrix, e.g., string or membrane order parameters.
These phases are different from the mixed-state phases characterized by their information theoretical properties, such as the decohered SPT states originally protected by higher-form symmetry
Raussendorf et al. 2005
;
Roberts and Bartlett 2020
;
Lee et al. 2022
and the decohered topologically ordered states
Bao et al. 2023
;
Fan et al. 2023
;
Lee et al. 2023
, which can change under a non-degenerate local channel circuit and may still require “two-way” connection in the definition
Sang et al. 2023
.
It remains open whether one can generalize the tensor network formulation to describe non-linear functions of the density matrix and characterize the information theoretical phases in mixed states.

Our results also open several directions for future study.
First, one can apply our framework to study the mixed-state SPT phases protected by a non-direct product of the weak and the strong symmetry, which may exhibit a richer phase structure
Ma et al. 2023
.
Moreover, it is worth generalizing our formulation to study the fermionic SPT phases in mixed states, which are of a different classification.

Ma and Turzillo 2024
discusses the constraints on the possible SPT phases in the doubled state. Ref.
Wang and Li 2024
argues that (2+1)d SPT mixed states with different anomalies cannot be connected using finite-depth symmetric channel circuits. Ref.

for informing us of their work in advance.

Appendix A
Local non-degenerate channel preserves strong injectivity

In this appendix, we prove that a local channel
\mathcal{N}
(described by a channel circuit of finite depth as shown in Fig.
3
) preserves the strong injectivity of MPDO
\rho
, i.e.
\rho
is strong injective if and only if
\mathcal{N}[\rho]
is strong injective.

Before we delve into the mathematical proof.
In pure ground states, we have a similar statement that local unitary circuits preserve the injectivity of MPS.
The statement holds because the exponentially decaying connected correlation functions cannot change dramatically under local unitaries due to the Lieb-Robinson bound.
Alternatively, one can also prove it based on the parent Hamiltonian
Perez-Garcia et al. 2007
.
Here, we prove that a non-degenerate local channel circuit preserves condition 2 in Def.
III.1
based on the correlation functions in the state.
Showing that the non-degenerate local channel also preserves condition 1 is much more involved.
When we treat the MPDO as an MPS in the doubled space, quantum channels act as non-unitary operations.
This results in a doubled state with a non-Hermitian parent Hamiltonian and can change the correlation functions dramatically.
However, the characteristics of injectivity only involve the linear structure of the Hilbert space but not the inner product.
We here modify the proof in Ref.
Perez-Garcia et al. 2007
to avoid considering the parent Hamiltonian and prove that the injectivity is preserved when local unitaries are replaced by local injective maps.

First, we consider the condition 2 in Def.
III.1
.
We show that the transfer matrix
\mathbb{T}
of
\mathcal{N}[\rho]
has a unique largest eigenvalue if and only if
\mathbb{T}
of
\rho
has a unique largest eigenvalue.
To prove this statement, the physical connected correlation function
\mathcal{N}[\rho]
takes the form

\displaystyle\tr(\calN[\rho] O_x O_{x'})-\tr(\calN[\rho] O_x)\tr(\calN[\rho] O_{x'})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tr(\rho\calN^\dagger[O_x O_{x'}])-\tr(\rho\calN^\dagger[O_x])\tr(\rho\calN^\dagger[O_{x'}])

(68)

where
\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}[\cdot]:=\sum_{i}K_{i}^{\dagger}(\cdot)K_{i}
is the adjoint channel.
For a local channel circuit,
\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}[O]
has local support.
When the separation
|x-x^{\prime}|
is sufficiently greater than the circuit depth,
\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}[O_{x}O_{x^{\prime}}]=\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}[O_{x}]\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}[O_{x^{\prime}}]
.
Since
\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}
is non-degenerate, all the connected correlations in
\rho
are exponentially decaying if and only if the same is true for correlations in
\mathcal{N}[\rho]
.

Next, to address condition 1 in Def.
III.1
, we prove that the injectivity of MPS is preserved under a local circuit consisting of not only unitary maps but also invertible linear maps.
This implies that the doubled state
|\rho\rangle\!\rangle
remains an injective MPS after applying a local channel circuit.

To begin, we introduce a concept called
locally reconstructible state
.
15
15

15

Mathematically, constructions below fit the concepts of presheaf and Galois connection. Here, we only present minimum definitions for simplicity.

Consider a given quantum state
\ket{\Psi}\in H_{\Lambda}
, where
H_{\Lambda}
is the Hilbert space of a one-dimensional spin chain
\Lambda
of size
L
.
We define a subspace
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)\subset H_{\Lambda}
as the image of reduced density matrix on subset
U
, i.e.
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi):=\imaginary\tr_{\Lambda-U}\outerproduct{\Psi}{\Psi}
.
The subspace
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)
represents the space of possible quantum states on the subsystem
U
when the entire system is in quantum state
\ket{\Psi}
.

Definition A.1
.

Consider a quantum state
\ket{\Psi}\in H_{\Lambda}
. The state
\ket{\Psi}
is locally reconstructible with a reconstructible length (not greater than)
d
if
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Phi)\subset\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)
for any subregion
U
of size (not greater than)
d
has a unique nonzero solution
\ket{\Phi}=\ket{\Psi}
.

We note that among all solutions to
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Phi)\subset\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)
, there is the largest element
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)\otimes H_{\Lambda-U}
, which includes all the other solutions as its subspace.
Thus, the condition for a locally reconstructible state can be equivalently formulated as
\ket{\Psi}=\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}{(\Psi)}}
, and we call
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}{(\Psi)}}
the closure of
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)
on a family of sets
16
16

16

We use same notation for a state
\Psi
and its corresponding one-dimensional subspace.
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}{(\Psi)}}:=\bigcap_{U}\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)\otimes H_{\Lambda-U},

(69)

where
U
runs over all the interval of length no greater than
d
.

Physically, a locally reconstructible state is fully determined by the reduced density matrices on subsets of size smaller than
d
.
According to this definition, the state with a reconstructible length
d=1
is an unentangled product state, such as
\ket{00\cdots 0}
,
, while a cat state
(\ket{00\cdots 0}+\ket{11\cdots 1})/\sqrt{2}
is not locally reconstructible.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}:H_{\Lambda}\mapsto H_{\Lambda}
be a local invertible map (arranged in a brickwork circuit).
\ket{\Psi}
is locally reconstructible if and only if
\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi}
is locally reconstructible.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{E}
is invertible, it is sufficient to prove that
\ket{\Psi}
being locally reconstructible implies that
\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi}
is locally reconstructible.
The key observation here is for any operator
O
supported in a finite region
d
,
\mathcal{E}O\mathcal{E}^{-1}
supports on an interval of length no greater than
d+2t
, where
t
is the circuit depth of the invertible map
\mathcal{E}
.

Let
d
be the reconstructible length of
\ket{\Psi}
.
For every interval
U
of length
d
, we define a projection operator
P_{U}
with image
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)\otimes H_{\Lambda-U}=\imaginary(P_{U}\otimes 1_{X-U}).

(70)

Appling
\mathcal{E}
to the both sides of the equation, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)\otimes H_{\Lambda-U})=\imaginary(\mathcal{E}(P_{U}\otimes 1_{\Lambda-U})\mathcal{E}^{-1}).

(71)

On the right hand side, the invertible map acts on the projection operator yields
\mathcal{E}(P_{U}\otimes 1_{\Lambda-U})\mathcal{E}^{-1}=Q_{U}\otimes 1_{\Lambda-V_{U}}
, where
Q_{U}
is supported on an interval
V_{U}
with length
d+2t
.
We denote
\imaginary Q_{U}=Z_{U}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)\otimes H_{\Lambda-U})=Z_{U}\otimes H_{\Lambda-V_{U}}.

(72)

Because
\ket{\Psi}\subset\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)\otimes H_{\Lambda-U}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{V_{U}}(\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi})\subset\mathcal{L}_{V_{U}}\left(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)\otimes H_{\Lambda-U})\right)=Z_{U}.

(73)

Taking the intersection of all
U
yields

\displaystyle\bigcap_{U}\mathcal{L}_{V_{U}}(\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi})\otimes H_{\Lambda-V_{U}}\subset\bigcap_{U}Z_{U}\otimes H_{\Lambda-V_{U}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{U}\mathcal{E}\left(\mathcal{L}_{U}(\ket{\Psi})\otimes H_{\Lambda-U}\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\bigcap_{U}\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)\otimes H_{\Lambda-U}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi}.

(74)

Considering that
\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi}\subset\mathcal{L}_{V_{U}}(\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi})\otimes H_{\Lambda-V_{U}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi}=\bigcap_{U}\mathcal{L}_{V_{U}}(\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi})\otimes H_{\Lambda-V_{U}},

(75)

which indicates that
\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi}
is locally reconstructible, and the reconstructible length is not greater than
d+2t
.
∎

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\ket{\Psi[A]}
be a translationally invariant MPS. The reconstructible length of
\ket{\Psi}
is not greater than
2
.

Proof.

The MPS is in the form

\displaystyle\Psi^{i_{1}\cdots i_{L}}=\tr(A^{i_1}A^{i_2}\cdots A^{i_L}).

(76)

Assume
\Phi
is a state satisfying
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Phi)\subset\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)
for all interval of length
2
.
First, we take
U=[1,2]
, because
A^{i_{1}}A^{i_{2}}
is an injective map from the virtual to the physical space,
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Phi)\subset\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)
indicates the existence of
X
such that
\Phi=\operatorname{tr}A^{i_{1}}A^{i_{2}}X^{i_{3}\cdots i_{L}}
.
Similarly, for
U=[2,3]
, there exist
Y
such that
\Phi=\tr A^{i_{2}}A^{i_{3}}Y^{i_{4}\cdots i_{L}i_{1}}
.
Since
A^{i_{2}}
is injective, we have
X^{i_{3}\cdots i_{L}}A^{i_{1}}=A^{i_{3}}Y^{i_{4}\cdots i_{L}i_{1}}
.
Because
A
is injective, there exists
A^{-1}
such that
\sum_{i_{1}}A^{i_{1}}(A^{-1})^{i_{1}}=\mathds{1}
on the virtual bond.
So
X^{i_{3}\cdots i_{L}}=\sum_{i_{1}}A^{i_{3}}Y^{i_{1}i_{4}\cdots i_{L}}(A^{-1})^{i_{1}}
.
Define
Z^{i_{4}\cdots i_{L}}:=\sum_{i_{1}}Y^{i_{1}i_{4}\cdots i_{L}}(A^{i_{1}})^{i_{1}}
, we then have
\Phi^{i_{1}\cdots i_{L}}=\tr A^{i_{1}}A^{i_{2}}A^{i_{3}}Z^{i_{4}\cdots i_{L}}
.
We can repeat this process and obtain the final result
\Phi^{i_{1}\cdots i_{L}}=\lambda\tr(A^{i_1}A^{i_2}\cdots A^{i_L})
, where
\lambda
is a phase factor.
Thus,
\ket{\Psi}
has a local reconstructible length not greater than
2
.
∎

Theorem A.2
.

Let
\ket{\Psi}
be a translationally invariant MPS. The dimension of
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\Psi)}
for large enough
d
is given by the number of normal bases in the canonical form of
\ket{\Psi}
.

Proof.

Let
k
be the number of normal bases in the canonical form of MPS
\Psi
. We have the following decomposition
\Psi=\sum_{\mu=1}^{k}\Psi_{\mu}
, where every
\Psi_{\mu}
can be written as a normal MPS.
According to the theory of MPS, the normal bases are linearly independent in the physical space after grouping a finite number of sites
Cirac et al. 2021
.
This is to say for an interval
U
of sufficiently large length
d
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi)=\oplus_{\mu=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{U}(\Psi_{\mu})
.
So
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}{(\Psi)}}=\oplus_{\mu=1}^{k}\mathbb{C}\Psi_{\mu}
, which has dimension
k
.
∎

Finally, we prove the key result that the brickwork circuit of invertible maps preserves the injectivity (normality) of MPS.

Theorem A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{E}:H_{\Lambda}\mapsto H_{\Lambda}
be a translationally invariant invertible local map. Then, the number of normal bases of
\ket{\Psi}
is invariant under
\mathcal{E}
. In particular,
\mathcal{E}
maps a normal MPS to a normal MPS.

Proof.

According to the previous theorem, the number of normal bases is the dimension of
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\Psi)}
.
Because
\mathcal{E}
preserves the local reconstructibility of the state,
\mathcal{E}(\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\Psi)})
is closed, namely
\mathcal{E}(\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\Psi)})=\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\mathcal{E}(\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\Psi)}))}
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\Psi)}=\oplus_{\mu=1}^{k}\mathbb{C}\Psi_{\mu}
, we have
\mathcal{E}(\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\Psi)})=\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi})}
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi})}=\dim\mathcal{E}(\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\Psi)})=\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}_{d}(\Psi)},

(77)

which indicates the number of normal bases in
\mathcal{E}\ket{\Psi}
and
\ket{\Psi}
are the same.
∎

Appendix B
Basics of group cohomology

Here, we briefly review the basic concepts in group cohomology.
Given a group
G
, let
\omega_{n}(g_{1},\cdots,g_{n})
be a function of
n
group elements of
G
and take the value in the
G
-module denoted by
M
.
Such a function is called an
n
-cochain, and we use
\mathcal{C}^{n}(G,M)
to denote the space of
n
-cochains.
We define a series of map
d_{n}:\mathcal{C}^{n}(G,M)\mapsto\mathcal{C}^{n+1}(G,M)
as

\displaystyle d\omega_{n}

\displaystyle(g_{1:n+1})

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle{}^{g_{1}}\!\omega_{n}(g_{2:n+1})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{n}^{(-1)^{i}}(g_{1:i-1},g_{i}g_{i+1},g_{i+2:n+1})

\displaystyle\omega_{n}^{(-1)^{n+1}}(g_{1:n}),

(78)

where we use the shorthand notation
g_{1:i}
to denote a set of group elements
(g_{1},g_{2},\cdots,g_{i})
in the argument of
\omega_{n}
.
The map satisfies
d\cdot d=1
.
We introduce the following definitions

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{n}(G,M)

\displaystyle:=\{\omega_{n}|d_{n}\omega_{n}=1,\omega_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(G,M)\},

(79)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{n}(G,M)

\displaystyle:=\{\omega_{n}|\omega_{n}\!=\!d_{n-1}\omega_{n-1},\omega_{n-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{n-1}(G,M)\},

where
\mathcal{Z}^{n}(G,M)
and
\mathcal{B}^{n}(G,M)
are the group of
n
-cocycles and
n
-coboundaries, respectively.
They are abelian groups and satisfies
\mathcal{B}^{n}(G,M)\subset\mathcal{Z}^{n}(G,M)
.
The
n
-th cohomology group is defined as
\mathcal{H}^{n}(G,M):=\mathcal{Z}^{n}(G,M)/\mathcal{B}^{n}(G,M)
.
For an
n
-cocycle
\omega_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
, we denote its cohomology class as
[\omega_{n}]
.

In this paper, we focus on the cases of
n=2,3
and consider
M
being a
G
-module
\mathrm{U}_{T}(1)
.
The group element
g\in G
acts on
a\in U_{T}(1)
as
{}^{g}\!a=a
and
a^{*}
for
g
being unitary and anti-unitary, respectively.
A
2
-cochain
\omega
is a
2
-cocycle if the following equation [Eq. (
11
)] holds for
g_{1,2,3}\in G
,

\displaystyle\omega(g_{1},g_{2})\omega(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})=\omega(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3}){}^{g_{1}}\!\omega(g_{2},g_{3}).

(80)

The
2
-cochain
\omega
is a
2
-coboundary if there exists
\varphi_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(G,U_{T}(1))
such that

\displaystyle\omega(g_{1},g_{2})=\frac{\varphi_{1}(g_{1}){}^{g_{1}}\!\varphi_{1}(g_{2})}{\varphi_{1}(g_{1}g_{2})}.

(81)

For
n=3
, the 3-cochain
\omega
is a 3-cocycle if the following equation [Eq. (
47
)] holds

\displaystyle\frac{\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\omega(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3},g_{4}){}^{g_{1}}\!\omega(g_{2},g_{3},g_{4})}{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3},g_{4})\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}g_{4})}=1.

(82)

The 3-cochain
\omega
is a 3-coboundary if there exists
\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(G,U_{T}(1))
such that

\displaystyle\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})=\frac{\varphi_{2}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3}){}^{g_{1}}\!\varphi_{2}(g_{2},g_{3})}{\varphi_{2}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})\varphi_{2}(g_{1},g_{2})},

(83)

for
g_{1,2,3,4}\in G
.

Appendix C
Decomposition of cocycles and the Künneth formula

The
n
-th cohomology of a unitary symmetry group given by a direct product
G\times H
takes a direct sum decomposition according to the Künneth formula

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(G\times H,\mathrm{U}(1))=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{n}\mathcal{H}^{n-k}(G,\mathcal{H}^{k}(H,\mathrm{U}(1))).

(84)

Accordingly, one may decompose the representative
n
-cocycle for the element of
\mathcal{H}^{n}(G\times H,U(1))
as the product
\prod_{k}\nu_{k}
, where each
\nu_{k}
is a representative cocycle for the element of
\mathcal{H}^{n-k}(G,\mathcal{H}^{k}(H,\mathrm{U}(1)))
. To facilitate the discussion we define the slant product.
The slant product is a mapping from
n
-cochain to
(n\,{-}\,1)
-cochain defined as

\displaystyle i_{g}\omega(g_{1},...,g_{n-1}):=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\omega(g_{1},...,g_{j},g,g_{j+1},...,g_{n-1})^{(-1)^{n-1+j}}

(85)

where
\omega
is a
n
-cochain. It is easy to check that this is also a mapping from
n
-cocycle to
(n\,{-}\,1)
-cocycle since
d(i_{g}\omega)=i_{g}(d\omega)
, and thus if
d\omega=0
, then
d(i_{g}\omega)=0
.
Accordingly, this is a group homomorphism from
\mathcal{H}^{n}
to
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}
.

In what follows, we explicitly demonstrate this decomposition for
n\,{=}\,2
and
3
using the slant product.

C.1
n=2

Using 2-cocycle condition
\omega(a,b)\omega(ab,c)=\omega(b,c)\omega(a,bc)
, we can show that

\displaystyle\omega(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(g_{1},g_{2}h_{1}h_{2})\omega(h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{1},h_{1})}=\frac{\omega(h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{1},h_{1})}\frac{\omega(g_{1},g_{2})\omega(g_{1}g_{2},h_{1}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{2},h_{1}h_{2})}

\displaystyle=\omega(g_{1},g_{2})\omega(h_{1},h_{2})\frac{\omega(h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{1},h_{1})}\frac{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},h_{1}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{2}h_{1},h_{2})\omega(g_{2},h_{1})}

\displaystyle=\omega(g_{1},g_{2})\omega(h_{1},h_{2})\frac{\omega(h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{1},h_{1})}\frac{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},h_{1}h_{2})}{\omega(h_{1}g_{2},h_{2})\omega(g_{2},h_{1})}

\displaystyle=\omega(g_{1},g_{2})\frac{\omega(h_{1},g_{2})}{\omega(g_{2},h_{1})}\omega(h_{1},h_{2})\frac{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},h_{1}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{1},h_{1})\omega(g_{2},h_{2})}.

(86)

By taking a gauge transformation
V_{x}\mapsto\varphi(x)V_{x}
with
\varphi(gh):=\omega(g,h)^{-1}
, we get

\displaystyle\omega\mapsto\omega\cdot\left(\frac{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},h_{1}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{1},h_{1})\omega(g_{2},h_{2})}\right)^{-1}

(87)

and the last bracket can be removed. Here, we used the direct product structure of
G\times H
. Alternatively, this can be derived by considering two equivalent ways to combine the projective representation of
g_{1},h_{1},g_{2},h_{2}
:

\displaystyle V_{g_{1}}V_{h_{1}}V_{g_{2}}V_{h_{2}}

\displaystyle=\omega(g_{1},h_{1})\omega(g_{2},h_{2})V_{g_{1}h_{1}}V_{g_{2}h_{2}}=\omega(g_{1},h_{1})\omega(g_{2},h_{2})\omega(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})V_{g_{1}g_{2}h_{1}h_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(h_{1},g_{2})}{\omega(g_{2},h_{1})}V_{g_{1}}V_{g_{2}}V_{h_{1}}V_{h_{2}}=\frac{\omega(h_{1},g_{2})}{\omega(g_{2},h_{1})}\omega(g_{1},g_{2})\omega(h_{1},h_{2})\omega(g_{1}g_{2},h_{1}h_{2})V_{g_{1}g_{2}h_{1}h_{2}}

(88)

Accordingly,
\omega\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(G\times H,\mathrm{U}(1))
is decomposed into three terms, each of which belongs to a certain term in the Kunneth decomposition as the following:

\displaystyle\nu_{0}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle:=\omega(g_{1},g_{2}),\qquad\nu_{1}(h_{1}|g_{2}):=\frac{\omega(h_{1},g_{2})}{\omega(g_{2},h_{1})}=i_{g_{2}}\omega(h_{1}),\qquad\nu_{2}(h_{1},h_{2}):=\omega(h_{1},h_{2})

(89)

Furthermore, note that the first group cohomology is nothing but the possible homomorphism from group
G
to
\mathrm{U}(1)
, i.e., abelianization of
G
since
f(g_{1})f(g_{2})=f(g_{1}g_{2})=f(g_{2})f(g_{1})
as a
\mathrm{U}(1)
number. We can show that
\nu_{1}(h|g)=i_{g}(h)
for a fixed
h
is a proper group homomorphism from
G
to
\mathrm{U}(1)
, i.e.,
i_{g_{1}}(h)\cdot i_{g_{2}}(h)=i_{g_{1}g_{2}}(h)
:

\displaystyle\frac{\omega(g_{1},h)}{\omega(h,g_{1})}\frac{\omega(g_{2},h)}{\omega(h,g_{2})}\frac{\omega(h,g_{1}g_{2})}{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},h)}=\frac{\omega(g_{2},h)\omega(hg_{1},g_{2})}{\omega(h,g_{2})\omega(g_{1},g_{2}h)}=1,

(90)

and
\nu_{1}(h|g)
is a 1-cochain for
H
on the module
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G,\mathrm{U}(1))
, i.e.,
[\nu_{1}(h|g)]\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(H,\mathcal{H}^{1}(G,\mathrm{U}(1)))
. Therefore, each equivalence class of
[\nu_{k}]
is a faithful representation of
\mathcal{H}^{2-k}(G,\mathcal{H}^{k}(H,\mathrm{U}(1)))
for
k=0,1,2
.

Note that this decomposition can be performed successively. If a given group is
G\times H\times K
, one can first decompose based on Eq. (
89
) between
G
and
H\times K
, and then further apply this decomposition for
\nu_{1}(h_{1}k_{1}|g_{2})
and
\nu_{2}(h_{1}k_{1},h_{2}k_{2})
as follows:

\displaystyle\nu_{1}(h_{1}k_{1}|g_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(h_{1}k_{1},g_{2})}{\omega(g_{2},h_{1}k_{1})}=\frac{\omega(h_{1},g_{2})}{\omega(g_{2},h_{1})}\frac{\omega(k_{1},g_{2})}{\omega(g_{2},k_{1})}=\nu_{1}(h_{1}|g_{2})\cdot\nu_{1}(k_{1}|g_{2})

\displaystyle\nu_{2}(h_{1}k_{1},h_{2}k_{2})

\displaystyle=\omega(h_{1}k_{1},h_{2}k_{2})=\nu_{0}(h_{1},h_{2})\cdot\nu_{1}(h_{1}|k_{2})\cdot\nu_{2}(k_{1},k_{2})

(91)

where
\nu_{1}(x|y)=i_{y}\omega(x)
. In the first line, we used the 2-cocycle consistency equation. Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(G\times H\times K,\mathrm{U}(1))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{2}(G,\mathrm{U}(1))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{2}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{2}(H,\mathrm{U}(1))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(G,\mathcal{H}^{1}(K,\mathrm{U}(1)))

\displaystyle\quad\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(G,\mathcal{H}^{1}(H,\mathrm{U}(1)))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(H,\mathcal{H}^{1}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))),

(92)

six
\nu_{k}
s gives the proper decomposition of
\omega
into the representative elements of the six Kunneth components.

C.2
n=3

Using 3-cocycle condition
\omega(a,b,c)\omega(a,bc,d)\omega(b,c,d)=\omega(ab,c,d)\omega(a,b,cd)
, we can show that

\displaystyle\omega(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2},g_{3}h_{3})

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=0}^{3}\nu_{k}(h_{1},...,h_{k}|g_{k+1},...,g_{3})

\displaystyle\nu_{0}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})

\displaystyle:=\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(G,\mathrm{U}(1))

\displaystyle\nu_{1}(h_{1}|g_{2},g_{3})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\omega(h_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\omega(g_{2},g_{3},h_{1})}{\omega(g_{2},h_{1},g_{3})}=i_{h_{1}}\omega(g_{2},g_{3})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(G,\mathcal{H}^{1}(H,\mathrm{U}(1)))

\displaystyle\nu_{2}(h_{1},h_{2}|g_{3})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\omega(h_{1},h_{2},g_{3})\omega(g_{3},h_{1},h_{2})}{\omega(h_{1},g_{3},h_{2})}=i_{g_{3}}\omega(h_{1},h_{2})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(G,\mathcal{H}^{2}(H,\mathrm{U}(1)))\simeq\mathcal{H}^{2}(H,\mathcal{H}^{1}(G,\mathrm{U}(1)))

\displaystyle\nu_{3}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})

\displaystyle:=\omega(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\in\mathcal{H}^{0}(G,\mathcal{H}^{3}(H,\mathrm{U}(1)))\simeq\mathcal{H}^{3}(H,\mathrm{U}(1)).

(93)

up to 3-coboundary terms. To show this, let us define several coboundary terms as follows

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})

\displaystyle:=\omega(g_{1},h_{1},g_{2}h_{2}),\quad d\varphi_{1}(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2},g_{3}h_{3})=\frac{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},h_{1}h_{2},g_{3}h_{3})\omega(g_{1},h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{1},h_{1},g_{2}g_{3}h_{2}h_{3})\omega(g_{2},h_{2},g_{3}h_{3})}

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})

\displaystyle:=\omega(g_{1},g_{2},h_{1}h_{2})^{-1},\quad d\varphi_{2}(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2},g_{3}h_{3})=\frac{\omega(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3},h_{1}h_{2}h_{3})\omega(g_{1},g_{2},h_{1}h_{2})}{\omega(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3},h_{1}h_{2}h_{3})\omega(g_{2},g_{3},h_{2}h_{3})}

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})

\displaystyle:=\omega(h_{1},g_{2},h_{2})^{-1},\quad d\varphi_{3}(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2},g_{3}h_{3})=\frac{\omega(h_{2},g_{3},h_{3})\omega(h_{1},g_{2}g_{3},h_{2}h_{3})}{\omega(h_{1},g_{2},h_{3})\omega(h_{1}h_{2},g_{3},h_{3})}

\displaystyle\varphi_{4}(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2})

\displaystyle:=\omega(g_{2},h_{1},h_{2}),\quad d\varphi_{4}(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2},g_{3}h_{3})=\frac{\omega(g_{2},h_{1},h_{2})\omega(g_{3},h_{1}h_{2},h_{3})}{\omega(g_{3},h_{2},h_{3})\omega(g_{2}g_{3},h_{1},h_{2}h_{3})}.

(94)

Then, we can use the 3-cocycle condition successively to explicitly show that

\displaystyle\omega(g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2},g_{3}h_{3})

\displaystyle=\quantity[ \prod_{k=0}^3 \nu_k(h_1,...,h_k | g_{k+1},..., g_3) ]\cdot d\varphi_{1}d\varphi_{2}d\varphi_{3}d\varphi_{4}

(95)

Furthermore, note that for fixed
(g_{2},g_{3})
:

\displaystyle\frac{i_{h_{1}}\omega(g_{2},g_{3})\cdot i_{h_{2}}\omega(g_{2},g_{3})}{i_{h_{1}h_{2}}\omega(g_{2},g_{3})}=1

(96)

and
\nu_{1}(h_{1}|g_{2},g_{3})\,{=}\,i_{h_{1}}\omega(g_{2},g_{3})
is a 2-cochain for
G
(since
di_{g}\omega=i_{g}d\omega=0
) on the module
\mathcal{H}^{1}(H,\mathrm{U}(1))
, i.e.,
[\nu_{1}(h_{1}|g_{2},g_{3})]\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(G,\mathcal{H}^{1}(H,\mathrm{U}(1)))
. Similarly,
\nu_{2}(h_{1},h_{2}|g_{3})\,{=}\,i_{g_{3}}\omega(h_{1},h_{2})
is a 2-cochain for
H
on the module
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G,\mathrm{U}(1))
. Therefore, each equivalence class of
[\nu_{k}]
is a faithful representation of
\mathcal{H}^{3-k}(G,\mathcal{H}^{k}(H,\mathrm{U}(1)))
for
k=0,1,2,3
. Again, this decomposition can be applied iteratively for a direct product of more than two groups.

Appendix D
Absence of mixed anomaly between
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{p}

In this section, we show the absence of mixed anomaly between the Hermiticity symmetry
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
and the physical symmetry
\mathbb{G}_{p}
in strong injective MPDO in 1d and strong semi-injective TNDO in 2d.

D.1
One dimension

Section
V.1
shows the absence of non-trivial SPT phase protected by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
, i.e.
\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=1
.
Here, we show that, by redefining the projective representation, one can trivialize the 2-cocycle involving the physical symmetry
\mathsf{g}\in\mathbb{G}_{p}
and the Hermitian conjugate
\mathbb{H}
, i.e.
\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})=1
.
This indicates the absence of mixed anomaly between
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{p}
.

To begin, the projective representation satisfies

\displaystyle V_{\mathsf{g}}V_{\mathbb{H}}=\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})V_{\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H}}=\frac{\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}})}V_{\mathbb{H}}V^{*}_{\overline{\mathsf{g}}},

(97)

where
\overline{\mathsf{g}}=\mathbb{H}\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H}
.
We consider a gauge transformation (i.e. redefinition) of the projective representation,

\displaystyle V_{\mathsf{g}}\mapsto\varphi(\mathsf{g})V_{\mathsf{g}},\quad V_{\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H}}\mapsto\varphi(\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H})V_{\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H}}.

(98)

The gauge transformation adds 2-coboundary to the 2-cocycle

\displaystyle\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})\mapsto\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})\frac{\varphi(\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H})}{\varphi(\mathsf{g})\varphi(\mathbb{H})}.

(99)

By picking a particular gauge

\begin{gathered}\varphi(\mathsf{g})=\sqrt{\frac{\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}})}},\quad\varphi(\overline{\mathsf{g}})=\sqrt{\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}},\mathbb{H})}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})}},\\
\varphi(\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}})}},\quad\varphi(\mathbb{H})=1,\end{gathered}

(100)

one obtain a trivial 2-cocycle
\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})=1
.
Here, we use the relation

\displaystyle V_{\mathbb{H}}^{-1}V_{\mathsf{g}}V_{\mathbb{H}}(V^{*}_{\overline{\mathsf{g}}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=V_{\mathbb{H}}^{*}V_{\mathsf{g}}(V_{\mathbb{H}}^{*})^{-1}(V_{\overline{\mathsf{g}}}^{*})^{-1}

(101)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\frac{\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}},\mathbb{H})}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})},

knowing that
V_{\mathbb{H}}^{*}V_{\mathbb{H}}=\mathds{1}
.
This result indicates the absence of mixed anomaly.

In the analysis of SPT phases protected by the physical symmetry
\mathbb{G}_{p}
, we work with the gauge choice with
\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})=1
.
We note that such choices are not unique.
In this partially fixed gauge, we have (Eq. (
13
))

\displaystyle\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})=\omega^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2}),

(102)

which one can show using the 2-cocycle condition in Eq. (
11
).

D.2
Two dimension

The reduction operators in 2d TNDO are defined up to a gauge transformation,

P_{g_{1},g_{2}}\mapsto\varphi(g_{1},g_{2})P_{g_{1},g_{2}},\text{ for }g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathbb{G}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}.

(103)

where
\varphi(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
is a
\mathrm{U}(1)
phase.
Accordingly, the 3-cocycle transforms as

\displaystyle\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})

\displaystyle\mapsto\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})d\varphi(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})

\displaystyle=\omega(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\frac{\varphi(g_{1},g_{2})\varphi(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})}{\varphi(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3}){}^{g_{1}}\!\varphi(g_{2},g_{3})},

(104)

where
d\varphi
is the derivative of the 2-cochain
\varphi
defined in Eq. (
78
).
Here, we show the existence of a gauge choice such that the 3-cocycle is trivial if one element is the Hermitian conjugation.
We further derive the constraint on 3-cocycle in this gauge due to the Hermiticity symmetry.

First, the anti-unitary
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
cannot protect non-trivial SPT phases in 2D
Chen et al. 2013
.
Specifically,
\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
transforms as

\displaystyle\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})

\displaystyle\mapsto\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})d\varphi(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})

\displaystyle=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\varphi(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})^{2}.

(105)

By picking a gauge
\varphi_{1}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=1/\sqrt{\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})}
, one obtains
\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=1
, indicating the absence of SPT phases protected by the anti-unitary
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
.

Next, we work with the gauge choice
\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=1
and discuss the possibility to set
\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})=1
for
\mathsf{g}\in\mathbb{G}_{p}
by further fixing the gauge.
In the case
\mathsf{g}\neq\overline{\mathsf{g}}
, we can choose the gauge

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}})}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}\right)^{1/4},

(106)

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}})}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}\right)^{1/4},

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})}\left(\frac{\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}})}\right)^{1/4},

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H}\mathsf{g})

\displaystyle=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})\left(\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}})}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}\right)^{1/4},

which sets
\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})=1
after the transformation
\omega\mapsto\omega\,d\varphi_{2}
.
Here, we use the relation

\displaystyle\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}})}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}=\frac{\omega(\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}},\mathbb{H})}{\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})},

(107)

which is derived directly from the
3
-cocycle condition in Eq. (
47
) and
\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=1
.

In the case
\mathsf{g}=\overline{\mathsf{g}}
, the cocycle can remain non-trivial, representing the mixed anomaly between
\mathbb{D}\cong G\times K\in\mathbb{G}_{p}
and the Hermiticity symmetry, characterized by
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{D},\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}},U_{T}(1)))
.
Here,
\mathbb{D}
involves the weak symmetry
K
and the diagonal symmetry in
G_{l}\times G_{r}
, in which each element transforms the ket and bra copy in the same way.
Such an SPT state is the condensate of domain walls of symmetry
\mathbb{D}
decorated by the 1d SPT states protected by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
symmetry.

Although these phases are allowed based on group cohomology, they cannot exist in strong semi-injective TNDO.
The anomaly in these states is in the nontrivial cocycles associated with the reduction operators for
V_{\mathsf{g}}
with
\mathsf{g}\in\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
as in Eq. (
45
).
Crucially, the transfer matrix
\mathbb{T}
(Eq. (
49
)) in strong semi-injective TNDO is invariant under the symmetry
\mathbb{D}
and transforms to its complex conjugate
\mathbb{T}^{*}
under
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
.
Hence, the eigenstate
|\phi[C])
associated with the unique largest eigenvalue of
\mathbb{T}
is invariant under
V_{\mathsf{g}}
for
\mathsf{g}\in\mathbb{D}
and transforms to
|\phi[C^{*}])
under
V_{\mathbb{H}}
.
Similar to the discussion leading to Eq. (
53
), this indicates that the 3-cocycle is coboundary, i.e.

\displaystyle\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2},\mathsf{g}_{3})=\frac{\varphi(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2}\mathsf{g}_{3}){}^{\mathsf{g}_{1}}\!\varphi(\mathsf{g}_{2},\mathsf{g}_{3})}{\varphi(\mathsf{g}_{1}\mathsf{g}_{2},\mathsf{g}_{3})\varphi(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})}

(108)

for elements
\mathsf{g}_{1,2,3}\in\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
.
Thus, one can always pick a gauge such that
\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})=1
in strong semi-injective TNDOs.

Last, we show the existence of a gauge choice to trivialize the 3-cocycle involving the Hermitian conjugate.
We work with the partially fixed gauge such that
\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})=1
, which requires the gauge transformation to satisfy

\begin{gathered}\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})^{2}=1,\quad\frac{\varphi_{3}(\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\varphi_{3}(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}{\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})}=1,\\
\frac{\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H}\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})\varphi_{3}(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})}{\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}\mathbb{H})}=1,\quad\frac{\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})}{\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H}\mathsf{g})}=1.\end{gathered}

(109)

One can obtain
\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2},\mathbb{H})=\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{2})=\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})=1
for
\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2}\in\mathbb{G}_{p}
by choosing the following gauge

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g})=\varphi_{3}(\mathsf{g},\mathbb{H})=1,

(110)

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{2})}{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})}\right)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(\mathbb{H}\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})}\left(\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})}{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{2})}\right)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2}\mathbb{H})

\displaystyle=\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2},\mathbb{H})\left(\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{2})}{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})}\right)^{1/2}.

Here, we use the relation

\displaystyle\frac{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{2})}{\omega(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2})}=\frac{\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2})}{\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2},\mathbb{H})\omega(\mathbb{H},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2})},

(111)

which can be derived from the 3-cocycle condition in Eq. (
47
).
Thus, we show the existence of a gauge choice to trivialize the cocycle involving Hermiticity symmetry, indicating the absence of mixed anomaly between
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\mathbb{H}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{p}
.

When classifying the SPT phases protected by the physical symmetry, we work with the gauge choice such that the 3-cocycle involving Hermitian conjugate is trivial.
The Hermiticity symmetry further imposes a constraint on the 3-cocycle (Eq. (
54
))

\displaystyle\omega(\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2},\mathsf{g}_{3})=\omega^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{1},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{2},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{3}),

(112)

which is again derived from the 3-cocycle condition in Eq. (
47
).
